Um… I guess what finally made me come in is this feeling that I’ve messed things up for everybody. Like I keep thinking I’ve ruined things, even when it’s probably stuff other people would just fix and move on from.

I’m in an apprenticeship, and lately I just feel like I’m fake being there, like I don’t actually deserve to be around people who know what they’re doing. And then I start thinking I’m letting my family down, and even the younger apprentices, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in my head. Like I’m supposed to be someone people can count on, and instead I’m just… disappointing everyone. That’s mostly why I’m here.

I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean, I do worry what people think of me sometimes, especially at work, because everyone seems like they know what they’re doing and I’m still learning. But I don’t think I’m, like, avoiding people because I’m scared they’ll reject me.

Like I keep thinking I’ve ruined things, even when it’s probably stuff other people would just fix and move on from.\n\nI’m in an apprenticeship, and lately I just feel like I’m fake being there, like I don’t actually deserve to be around people who know what they’re doing. And then I start thinking I’m letting my family down, and even the younger apprentices, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in my head. Like I’m supposed to be someone people can count on, and instead I’m just… disappointing everyone. That’s mostly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you tend to avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean, I do worry what people think of me sometimes, especially at work, because everyone seems like they know what they’re doing and I’m still learning. Yeah… I think it did start after a change, not like one huge dramatic thing, but when I started this sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.

Maybe stressed and kind of ashamed-feeling, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, even when you try to stop?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” honestly. I do worry, but it feels pretty centered around certain things — mostly whether I’m doing well enough, whether I’m falling behind, or whether I handled something wrong.

Like, if I make a mistake or I think I might’ve seemed unsure, I can get stuck on that and keep going over it in my head even when I’m trying not to. But I don’t know if I’m worrying excessively about everything, like money and health and my family and random disasters or whatever. It doesn’t really feel that broad.

It’s more like once my brain latches onto a specific thing, it keeps picking at it. I can tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this, you already went over it,” but then I’ll still come back to it. So… yes and no, I guess. I worry more than I want to, but I’m not sure it’s about a lot of different things.

Yeah… I think so. I hadn’t really called it that, but lately it’s like the stuff that normally makes me feel like me just kind of… doesn’t pull me in.

Like, I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and not even feel the usual little urge to sit down and draw something, even if I have time. And volleyball, too — I’ve skipped it a few times, and normally I’d feel disappointed missing it, or at least I’d miss seeing people and moving around, but lately it’s more like, “Eh, I guess I’m not going,” and I don’t feel much either way.

Same with the volunteer group texts. Those used to make me feel connected, like I was doing something useful outside of work and school stuff. Now I see the messages come in and I just ignore them, not even because I hate it or anything. It’s just like there’s no spark there. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it does feel like those things don’t hit the same right now.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess what finally made me come in is this feeling that I’ve messed things up for everybody. Like I keep thinking I’ve ruined things, even when it’s probably stuff other people would just fix and move on from.\n\nI’m in an apprenticeship, and lately I just feel like I’m fake being there, like I don’t actually deserve to be around people who know what they’re doing. And then I start thinking I’m letting my family down, and even the younger apprentices, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in my head. Like I’m supposed to be someone people can count on, and instead I’m just… disappointing everyone. That’s mostly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you tend to avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean, I do worry what people think of me sometimes, especially at work, because everyone seems like they know what they’re doing and I’m still learning. But I don’t think I’m, like, avoiding people because I’m scared they’ll reject me.\n\nI still go in, I still talk to people, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. It’s more that afterward I’ll replay things and wonder if I sounded stupid or if they think I don’t belong there. But I wouldn’t say I’m staying away from social stuff specifically because of that. At least… I haven’t really noticed myself doing that.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after a change, not like one huge dramatic thing, but when I started this sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.\n\nThat was about five months ago, I think. Before that I’d get stressed, obviously, because the apprenticeship is already a lot, but it didn’t feel like this constant pattern of questioning myself and feeling like I’m letting everyone down. Once I added the certification, it was like suddenly there was this extra layer of pressure, and I kept comparing myself to people who seemed more confident or further along.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say this whole thing has been going on for around five months, since I began that certification program.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “depressed” feels like a big word. I’ve definitely had days where I feel really low on myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or I can’t stop thinking I’m not measuring up. Then I can get pretty stuck in it. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m depressed most days. Maybe stressed and kind of ashamed-feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” honestly. I do worry, but it feels pretty centered around certain things — mostly whether I’m doing well enough, whether I’m falling behind, or whether I handled something wrong.\n\nLike, if I make a mistake or I think I might’ve seemed unsure, I can get stuck on that and keep going over it in my head even when I’m trying not to. But I don’t know if I’m worrying excessively about everything, like money and health and my family and random disasters or whatever. It doesn’t really feel that broad.\n\nIt’s more like once my brain latches onto a specific thing, it keeps picking at it. I can tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this, you already went over it,” but then I’ll still come back to it. So… yes and no, I guess. I worry more than I want to, but I’m not sure it’s about a lot of different things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I hadn’t really called it that, but lately it’s like the stuff that normally makes me feel like me just kind of… doesn’t pull me in.\n\nLike, I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and not even feel the usual little urge to sit down and draw something, even if I have time. And volleyball, too — I’ve skipped it a few times, and normally I’d feel disappointed missing it, or at least I’d miss seeing people and moving around, but lately it’s more like, “Eh, I guess I’m not going,” and I don’t feel much either way.\n\nSame with the volunteer group texts. Those used to make me feel connected, like I was doing something useful outside of work and school stuff. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it’s changed. It’s been kind of all over the place, actually.

For a while I was getting by on, like, three or four hours of sleep and somehow still showing up for work and class feeling… not tired exactly, more like wired. Like I’d be up way too late, sleep barely at all, and then still be moving the next day like my body was running on something else. I remember thinking, “Okay, this probably isn’t great,” but also I was still functioning, so I kind of brushed it off.

But lately it’s different. Now I’ll lie down and just not be able to fall asleep, even when I know I need to. Or I do fall asleep and then wake up before my alarm, and it feels like I didn’t actually rest. Like technically I was in bed, but my brain and body don’t feel reset at all. It’s frustrating because I’ll look at the clock and start calculating how much time I have left, which obviously doesn’t help. So yes, definitely my sleep hasn’t been normal.

But I don’t think I’m, like, avoiding people because I’m scared they’ll reject me.\n\nI still go in, I still talk to people, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. At least… I haven’t really noticed myself doing that.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after a change, not like one huge dramatic thing, but when I started this sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.\n\nThat was about five months ago, I think. Before that I’d get stressed, obviously, because the apprenticeship is already a lot, but it didn’t feel like this constant pattern of questioning myself and feeling like I’m letting everyone down. Once I added the certification, it was like suddenly there was this extra layer of pressure, and I kept comparing myself to people who seemed more confident or further along.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say this whole thing has been going on for around five months, since I began that certification program.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “depressed” feels like a big word. I’ve definitely had days where I feel really low on myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or I can’t stop thinking I’m not measuring up. But if you mean down all day, every day, or most days in a really clear way… I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it like that.\n\nIt’s more like it comes in waves, especially when I start thinking about work or the certification or whether people are noticing I’m behind. Then I can get pretty stuck in it. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m depressed most days. Maybe stressed and kind of ashamed-feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” honestly. I do worry, but it feels pretty centered around certain things — mostly whether I’m doing well enough, whether I’m falling behind, or whether I handled something wrong.\n\nLike, if I make a mistake or I think I might’ve seemed unsure, I can get stuck on that and keep going over it in my head even when I’m trying not to. But I don’t know if I’m worrying excessively about everything, like money and health and my family and random disasters or whatever. It doesn’t really feel that broad.\n\nIt’s more like once my brain latches onto a specific thing, it keeps picking at it. I can tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this, you already went over it,” but then I’ll still come back to it. So… yes and no, I guess. I worry more than I want to, but I’m not sure it’s about a lot of different things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I hadn’t really called it that, but lately it’s like the stuff that normally makes me feel like me just kind of… doesn’t pull me in.\n\nLike, I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and not even feel the usual little urge to sit down and draw something, even if I have time. And volleyball, too — I’ve skipped it a few times, and normally I’d feel disappointed missing it, or at least I’d miss seeing people and moving around, but lately it’s more like, “Eh, I guess I’m not going,” and I don’t feel much either way.\n\nSame with the volunteer group texts. Those used to make me feel connected, like I was doing something useful outside of work and school stuff. Now I see the messages come in and I just ignore them, not even because I hate it or anything. It’s just like there’s no spark there. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it does feel like those things don’t hit the same right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your sleep changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s changed. It’s been kind of all over the place, actually.\n\nFor a while I was getting by on, like, three or four hours of sleep and somehow still showing up for work and class feeling… not tired exactly, more like wired. Like I’d be up way too late, sleep barely at all, and then still be moving the next day like my body was running on something else. I remember thinking, “Okay, this probably isn’t great,” but also I was still functioning, so I kind of brushed it off.\n\nBut lately it’s different. Now I’ll lie down and just not be able to fall asleep, even when I know I need to. Or I do fall asleep and then wake up before my alarm, and it feels like I didn’t actually rest. Like technically I was in bed, but my brain and body don’t feel reset at all. It’s frustrating because I’ll look at the clock and start calculating how much time I have left, which obviously doesn’t help. So yes, definitely my sleep hasn’t been normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think “energized” is probably the right word, but it was more than just having a good day or feeling motivated. There were stretches — like weeks — where I felt almost too good, like everything was kind of clicking and I had this feeling like Miami was mine, like I could just move through the city and handle anything.

And at first it didn’t feel like a problem, because compared to feeling tired or stuck, it honestly felt kind of amazing. I’d be sleeping three or four hours and still feel like I had this extra charge in me. Like I didn’t need as much rest as everyone else, or like slowing down was the thing that would mess me up.

But it wasn’t just happy, either. That’s the part that makes me a little uncomfortable to admit. I was also way more touchy. If a coworker made some little comment, or if someone slowed me down when I was trying to get something done, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Like, I’d hear myself getting sharp, and part of me would be like, “Why are you reacting like that?” but in the moment it felt completely justified, like they were in my way.

So yeah, I guess I did feel unusually energized or high, but it came with this edge to it too. Like really up, but also really easy to set off.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.2, F32.1, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess what finally made me come in is this feeling that I’ve messed things up for everybody. Like I keep thinking I’ve ruined things, even when it’s probably stuff other people would just fix and move on from.\n\nI’m in an apprenticeship, and lately I just feel like I’m fake being there, like I don’t actually deserve to be around people who know what they’re doing. And then I start thinking I’m letting my family down, and even the younger apprentices, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in my head. Like I’m supposed to be someone people can count on, and instead I’m just… disappointing everyone. That’s mostly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you tend to avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean, I do worry what people think of me sometimes, especially at work, because everyone seems like they know what they’re doing and I’m still learning. But I don’t think I’m, like, avoiding people because I’m scared they’ll reject me.\n\nI still go in, I still talk to people, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. It’s more that afterward I’ll replay things and wonder if I sounded stupid or if they think I don’t belong there. But I wouldn’t say I’m staying away from social stuff specifically because of that. At least… I haven’t really noticed myself doing that.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after a change, not like one huge dramatic thing, but when I started this sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.\n\nThat was about five months ago, I think. Before that I’d get stressed, obviously, because the apprenticeship is already a lot, but it didn’t feel like this constant pattern of questioning myself and feeling like I’m letting everyone down. Once I added the certification, it was like suddenly there was this extra layer of pressure, and I kept comparing myself to people who seemed more confident or further along.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say this whole thing has been going on for around five months, since I began that certification program.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “depressed” feels like a big word. I’ve definitely had days where I feel really low on myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or I can’t stop thinking I’m not measuring up. But if you mean down all day, every day, or most days in a really clear way… I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it like that.\n\nIt’s more like it comes in waves, especially when I start thinking about work or the certification or whether people are noticing I’m behind. Then I can get pretty stuck in it. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m depressed most days. Maybe stressed and kind of ashamed-feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” honestly. I do worry, but it feels pretty centered around certain things — mostly whether I’m doing well enough, whether I’m falling behind, or whether I handled something wrong.\n\nLike, if I make a mistake or I think I might’ve seemed unsure, I can get stuck on that and keep going over it in my head even when I’m trying not to. But I don’t know if I’m worrying excessively about everything, like money and health and my family and random disasters or whatever. It doesn’t really feel that broad.\n\nIt’s more like once my brain latches onto a specific thing, it keeps picking at it. I can tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this, you already went over it,” but then I’ll still come back to it. So… yes and no, I guess. I worry more than I want to, but I’m not sure it’s about a lot of different things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I hadn’t really called it that, but lately it’s like the stuff that normally makes me feel like me just kind of… doesn’t pull me in.\n\nLike, I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and not even feel the usual little urge to sit down and draw something, even if I have time. And volleyball, too — I’ve skipped it a few times, and normally I’d feel disappointed missing it, or at least I’d miss seeing people and moving around, but lately it’s more like, “Eh, I guess I’m not going,” and I don’t feel much either way.\n\nSame with the volunteer group texts. Those used to make me feel connected, like I was doing something useful outside of work and school stuff. Now I see the messages come in and I just ignore them, not even because I hate it or anything. It’s just like there’s no spark there. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it does feel like those things don’t hit the same right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your sleep changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s changed. It’s been kind of all over the place, actually.\n\nFor a while I was getting by on, like, three or four hours of sleep and somehow still showing up for work and class feeling… not tired exactly, more like wired. Like I’d be up way too late, sleep barely at all, and then still be moving the next day like my body was running on something else. I remember thinking, “Okay, this probably isn’t great,” but also I was still functioning, so I kind of brushed it off.\n\nBut lately it’s different. Now I’ll lie down and just not be able to fall asleep, even when I know I need to. Or I do fall asleep and then wake up before my alarm, and it feels like I didn’t actually rest. Like technically I was in bed, but my brain and body don’t feel reset at all. It’s frustrating because I’ll look at the clock and start calculating how much time I have left, which obviously doesn’t help. So yes, definitely my sleep hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were sleeping only three or four hours, did you also feel unusually energized or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “energized” is probably the right word, but it was more than just having a good day or feeling motivated. There were stretches — like weeks — where I felt almost too good, like everything was kind of clicking and I had this feeling like Miami was mine, like I could just move through the city and handle anything.\n\nAnd at first it didn’t feel like a problem, because compared to feeling tired or stuck, it honestly felt kind of amazing. I’d be sleeping three or four hours and still feel like I had this extra charge in me. Like I didn’t need as much rest as everyone else, or like slowing down was the thing that would mess me up.\n\nBut it wasn’t just happy, either. That’s the part that makes me a little uncomfortable to admit. I was also way more touchy. If a coworker made some little comment, or if someone slowed me down when I was trying to get something done, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Like, I’d hear myself getting sharp, and part of me would be like, “Why are you reacting like that?” but in the moment it felt completely justified, like they were in my way.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I did feel unusually energized or high, but it came with this edge to it too. Like really up, but also really easy to set off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’d be sleeping three or four hours and still feel like I had this extra charge in me. Like I didn’t need as much rest as everyone else, or like slowing down was the thing that would mess me up.\n\nBut it wasn’t just happy, either. That’s the part that makes me a little uncomfortable to admit. I was also way more touchy. If a coworker made some little comment, or if someone slowed me down when I was trying to get something done, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Like, I’d hear myself getting sharp, and part of me would be like, “Why are you reacting like that?” but in the moment it felt completely justified, like they were in my way.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I did feel unusually energized or high, but it came with this edge to it too. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t think I would’ve called it “powerful” at the time, because that sounds kind of dramatic, but looking back, that’s basically what it was.

I had this really strong feeling like I could see solutions other people just weren’t seeing. Like in class or on job sites, when instructors or the senior guys were explaining how something should be done, I’d be sitting there thinking, “Okay, but why are we doing it that way when there’s obviously a better way?” And not just, like, a normal suggestion. It was more like I genuinely thought I could design something better than them — better than people who had been doing it for years.

There was this whole idea in my head that if people would just listen to me, I could change how every building in Miami handled water. Like I was right on the edge of figuring out something huge, and everyone else was moving too slow or being stuck in old habits. At the time it felt completely clear to me, like confidence, like certainty. Now saying it out loud, it sounds… kind of much. But yeah, during those stretches, I definitely felt like I could do things other people couldn’t.

I remember thinking, “Okay, this probably isn’t great,” but also I was still functioning, so I kind of brushed it off.\n\nBut lately it’s different. Now I’ll lie down and just not be able to fall asleep, even when I know I need to. Or I do fall asleep and then wake up before my alarm, and it feels like I didn’t actually rest. Like technically I was in bed, but my brain and body don’t feel reset at all. It’s frustrating because I’ll look at the clock and start calculating how much time I have left, which obviously doesn’t help. So yes, definitely my sleep hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were sleeping only three or four hours, did you also feel unusually energized or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “energized” is probably the right word, but it was more than just having a good day or feeling motivated. There were stretches — like weeks — where I felt almost too good, like everything was kind of clicking and I had this feeling like Miami was mine, like I could just move through the city and handle anything.\n\nAnd at first it didn’t feel like a problem, because compared to feeling tired or stuck, it honestly felt kind of amazing. I’d be sleeping three or four hours and still feel like I had this extra charge in me. Like I didn’t need as much rest as everyone else, or like slowing down was the thing that would mess me up.\n\nBut it wasn’t just happy, either. That’s the part that makes me a little uncomfortable to admit. I was also way more touchy. If a coworker made some little comment, or if someone slowed me down when I was trying to get something done, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Like, I’d hear myself getting sharp, and part of me would be like, “Why are you reacting like that?” but in the moment it felt completely justified, like they were in my way.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I did feel unusually energized or high, but it came with this edge to it too. Like really up, but also really easy to set off.\n\nDoctor: During those stretches when you felt especially energized, did you feel unusually confident or powerful, like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t think I would’ve called it “powerful” at the time, because that sounds kind of dramatic, but looking back, that’s basically what it was.\n\nI had this really strong feeling like I could see solutions other people just weren’t seeing. Like in class or on job sites, when instructors or the senior guys were explaining how something should be done, I’d be sitting there thinking, “Okay, but why are we doing it that way when there’s obviously a better way?” And not just, like, a normal suggestion. It was more like I genuinely thought I could design something better than them — better than people who had been doing it for years.\n\nThere was this whole idea in my head that if people would just listen to me, I could change how every building in Miami handled water. Like I was right on the edge of figuring out something huge, and everyone else was moving too slow or being stuck in old habits. At the time it felt completely clear to me, like confidence, like certainty. Now saying it out loud, it sounds… kind of much. But yeah, during those stretches, I definitely felt like I could do things other people couldn’t.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those energized stretches, did you do things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess what finally made me come in is this feeling that I’ve messed things up for everybody. Like I keep thinking I’ve ruined things, even when it’s probably stuff other people would just fix and move on from.\n\nI’m in an apprenticeship, and lately I just feel like I’m fake being there, like I don’t actually deserve to be around people who know what they’re doing. And then I start thinking I’m letting my family down, and even the younger apprentices, which sounds kind of dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in my head. Like I’m supposed to be someone people can count on, and instead I’m just… disappointing everyone. That’s mostly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you tend to avoid social situations because you’re afraid people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. I mean, I do worry what people think of me sometimes, especially at work, because everyone seems like they know what they’re doing and I’m still learning. But I don’t think I’m, like, avoiding people because I’m scared they’ll reject me.\n\nI still go in, I still talk to people, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. It’s more that afterward I’ll replay things and wonder if I sounded stupid or if they think I don’t belong there. But I wouldn’t say I’m staying away from social stuff specifically because of that. At least… I haven’t really noticed myself doing that.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did start after a change, not like one huge dramatic thing, but when I started this sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.\n\nThat was about five months ago, I think. Before that I’d get stressed, obviously, because the apprenticeship is already a lot, but it didn’t feel like this constant pattern of questioning myself and feeling like I’m letting everyone down. Once I added the certification, it was like suddenly there was this extra layer of pressure, and I kept comparing myself to people who seemed more confident or further along.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say this whole thing has been going on for around five months, since I began that certification program.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “depressed” feels like a big word. I’ve definitely had days where I feel really low on myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or I can’t stop thinking I’m not measuring up. But if you mean down all day, every day, or most days in a really clear way… I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it like that.\n\nIt’s more like it comes in waves, especially when I start thinking about work or the certification or whether people are noticing I’m behind. Then I can get pretty stuck in it. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m depressed most days. Maybe stressed and kind of ashamed-feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” honestly. I do worry, but it feels pretty centered around certain things — mostly whether I’m doing well enough, whether I’m falling behind, or whether I handled something wrong.\n\nLike, if I make a mistake or I think I might’ve seemed unsure, I can get stuck on that and keep going over it in my head even when I’m trying not to. But I don’t know if I’m worrying excessively about everything, like money and health and my family and random disasters or whatever. It doesn’t really feel that broad.\n\nIt’s more like once my brain latches onto a specific thing, it keeps picking at it. I can tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this, you already went over it,” but then I’ll still come back to it. So… yes and no, I guess. I worry more than I want to, but I’m not sure it’s about a lot of different things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I hadn’t really called it that, but lately it’s like the stuff that normally makes me feel like me just kind of… doesn’t pull me in.\n\nLike, I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and not even feel the usual little urge to sit down and draw something, even if I have time. And volleyball, too — I’ve skipped it a few times, and normally I’d feel disappointed missing it, or at least I’d miss seeing people and moving around, but lately it’s more like, “Eh, I guess I’m not going,” and I don’t feel much either way.\n\nSame with the volunteer group texts. Those used to make me feel connected, like I was doing something useful outside of work and school stuff. Now I see the messages come in and I just ignore them, not even because I hate it or anything. It’s just like there’s no spark there. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it does feel like those things don’t hit the same right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your sleep changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s changed. It’s been kind of all over the place, actually.\n\nFor a while I was getting by on, like, three or four hours of sleep and somehow still showing up for work and class feeling… not tired exactly, more like wired. Like I’d be up way too late, sleep barely at all, and then still be moving the next day like my body was running on something else. I remember thinking, “Okay, this probably isn’t great,” but also I was still functioning, so I kind of brushed it off.\n\nBut lately it’s different. Now I’ll lie down and just not be able to fall asleep, even when I know I need to. Or I do fall asleep and then wake up before my alarm, and it feels like I didn’t actually rest. Like technically I was in bed, but my brain and body don’t feel reset at all. It’s frustrating because I’ll look at the clock and start calculating how much time I have left, which obviously doesn’t help. So yes, definitely my sleep hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were sleeping only three or four hours, did you also feel unusually energized or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “energized” is probably the right word, but it was more than just having a good day or feeling motivated. There were stretches — like weeks — where I felt almost too good, like everything was kind of clicking and I had this feeling like Miami was mine, like I could just move through the city and handle anything.\n\nAnd at first it didn’t feel like a problem, because compared to feeling tired or stuck, it honestly felt kind of amazing. I’d be sleeping three or four hours and still feel like I had this extra charge in me. Like I didn’t need as much rest as everyone else, or like slowing down was the thing that would mess me up.\n\nBut it wasn’t just happy, either. That’s the part that makes me a little uncomfortable to admit. I was also way more touchy. If a coworker made some little comment, or if someone slowed me down when I was trying to get something done, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Like, I’d hear myself getting sharp, and part of me would be like, “Why are you reacting like that?” but in the moment it felt completely justified, like they were in my way.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I did feel unusually energized or high, but it came with this edge to it too. Like really up, but also really easy to set off.\n\nDoctor: During those stretches when you felt especially energized, did you feel unusually confident or powerful, like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Yeah… it has, and that’s probably the part I feel the most embarrassed about, honestly. Because I can explain away a lot of the internal stuff to myself, like, “Okay, I was just really motivated,” or “I had a lot of ideas,” but the work and school side is harder to ignore.

My instructors have had to come after me more than once about sketches I said I was going to finish. Like, layout sketches or planning stuff where I’d be so convinced I had this better version in my head, and then I’d start it, get pulled into another idea, revise it too much, or just not turn in what I was supposed to when I was supposed to. And then it makes me look careless, which I hate, because I’m not trying to be careless. I actually care a lot.

And with my foreman, it’s kind of similar. He’s had to chase me about things taking longer than they should, or work that’s not moving at the pace it needs to. Not because I don’t know how to do it, but because I get caught up in doing it my way or thinking through all these extra angles, and then the actual job slows down. It’s really frustrating to admit that out loud.

I’m scared, honestly, that I’m getting close to messing up the apprenticeship and the certification I worked really hard to get. Like, I don’t think everything is ruined, but I can feel that I’m pushing it. People are noticing. And that scares me, because this isn’t just a random job to me — I’ve put a lot into it, and I don’t want my behavior during those stretches to be the reason I lose ground.

It’s more like I can remember feeling sped up and very sure of myself, but I’m having a harder time separating whether I actually did anything risky from just feeling like I could.\n\nI might need you to give me examples of what you mean — like spending money, driving fast, quitting something, getting into situations I normally wouldn’t — because without that, I’m kind of scanning my memory and not landing on anything definite.\n\nDoctor: During those energized stretches, did your behavior cause problems at work, school, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, and that’s probably the part I feel the most embarrassed about, honestly. Because I can explain away a lot of the internal stuff to myself, like, “Okay, I was just really motivated,” or “I had a lot of ideas,” but the work and school side is harder to ignore.\n\nMy instructors have had to come after me more than once about sketches I said I was going to finish. Like, layout sketches or planning stuff where I’d be so convinced I had this better version in my head, and then I’d start it, get pulled into another idea, revise it too much, or just not turn in what I was supposed to when I was supposed to. And then it makes me look careless, which I hate, because I’m not trying to be careless. I actually care a lot.\n\nAnd with my foreman, it’s kind of similar. He’s had to chase me about things taking longer than they should, or work that’s not moving at the pace it needs to. Not because I don’t know how to do it, but because I get caught up in doing it my way or thinking through all these extra angles, and then the actual job slows down. It’s really frustrating to admit that out loud.\n\nI’m scared, honestly, that I’m getting close to messing up the apprenticeship and the certification I worked really hard to get. Like, I don’t think everything is ruined, but I can feel that I’m pushing it. People are noticing. And that scares me, because this isn’t just a random job to me — I’ve put a lot into it, and I don’t want my behavior during those stretches to be the reason I lose ground.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Then I can get pretty stuck in it. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m depressed most days. I do worry, but it feels pretty centered around certain things — mostly whether I’m doing well enough, whether I’m falling behind, or whether I handled something wrong.\n\nLike, if I make a mistake or I think I might’ve seemed unsure, I can get stuck on that and keep going over it in my head even when I’m trying not to. But I don’t know if I’m worrying excessively about everything, like money and health and my family and random disasters or whatever. I can tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this, you already went over it,” but then I’ll still come back to it. So… yes and no, I guess. I worry more than I want to, but I’m not sure it’s about a lot of different things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I hadn’t really called it that, but lately it’s like the stuff that normally makes me feel like me just kind of… doesn’t pull me in.\n\nLike, I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and not even feel the usual little urge to sit down and draw something, even if I have time. And volleyball, too — I’ve skipped it a few times, and normally I’d feel disappointed missing it, or at least I’d miss seeing people and moving around, but lately it’s more like, “Eh, I guess I’m not going,” and I don’t feel much either way.\n\nSame with the volunteer group texts. Like I’d be up way too late, sleep barely at all, and then still be moving the next day like my body was running on something else. Now I’ll lie down and just not be able to fall asleep, even when I know I need to. Or I do fall asleep and then wake up before my alarm, and it feels like I didn’t actually rest. And that scares me, because this isn’t just a random job to me — I’ve put a lot into it, and I don’t want my behavior during those stretches to be the reason I lose ground.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long did those energized stretches usually last?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This whole pattern has been going on for about five months, starting after I began the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… I’d say this whole pattern has been going on for about five months now. It wasn’t just one random week where I got fired up and then went back to normal.

It really started after I began the sustainable plumbing certification on top of my apprenticeship. That’s when I noticed these stretches of feeling really energized and locked in started happening more, or at least became obvious enough that other people were reacting to it. Before that, I don’t think I would’ve described it the same way.

So, yeah — about five months altogether. The energized periods kind of come in stretches within that, but the overall pattern started around then.

I’ve definitely had days where I feel really low on myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or I can’t stop thinking I’m not measuring up. Then I can get pretty stuck in it. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m depressed most days. Maybe stressed and kind of ashamed-feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” honestly. I do worry, but it feels pretty centered around certain things — mostly whether I’m doing well enough, whether I’m falling behind, or whether I handled something wrong.\n\nLike, if I make a mistake or I think I might’ve seemed unsure, I can get stuck on that and keep going over it in my head even when I’m trying not to. But I don’t know if I’m worrying excessively about everything, like money and health and my family and random disasters or whatever. It doesn’t really feel that broad.\n\nIt’s more like once my brain latches onto a specific thing, it keeps picking at it. I can tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this, you already went over it,” but then I’ll still come back to it. So… yes and no, I guess. I worry more than I want to, but I’m not sure it’s about a lot of different things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I hadn’t really called it that, but lately it’s like the stuff that normally makes me feel like me just kind of… doesn’t pull me in.\n\nLike, I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and not even feel the usual little urge to sit down and draw something, even if I have time. And volleyball, too — I’ve skipped it a few times, and normally I’d feel disappointed missing it, or at least I’d miss seeing people and moving around, but lately it’s more like, “Eh, I guess I’m not going,” and I don’t feel much either way.\n\nSame with the volunteer group texts. Those used to make me feel connected, like I was doing something useful outside of work and school stuff. Now I see the messages come in and I just ignore them, not even because I hate it or anything. It’s just like there’s no spark there. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it does feel like those things don’t hit the same right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your sleep changing lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s changed. It’s been kind of all over the place, actually.\n\nFor a while I was getting by on, like, three or four hours of sleep and somehow still showing up for work and class feeling… not tired exactly, more like wired. Like I’d be up way too late, sleep barely at all, and then still be moving the next day like my body was running on something else. I remember thinking, “Okay, this probably isn’t great,” but also I was still functioning, so I kind of brushed it off.\n\nBut lately it’s different. Now I’ll lie down and just not be able to fall asleep, even when I know I need to. Or I do fall asleep and then wake up before my alarm, and it feels like I didn’t actually rest. Like technically I was in bed, but my brain and body don’t feel reset at all. It’s frustrating because I’ll look at the clock and start calculating how much time I have left, which obviously doesn’t help. So yes, definitely my sleep hasn’t been normal.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were sleeping only three or four hours, did you also feel unusually energized or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “energized” is probably the right word, but it was more than just having a good day or feeling motivated. There were stretches — like weeks — where I felt almost too good, like everything was kind of clicking and I had this feeling like Miami was mine, like I could just move through the city and handle anything.\n\nAnd at first it didn’t feel like a problem, because compared to feeling tired or stuck, it honestly felt kind of amazing. I’d be sleeping three or four hours and still feel like I had this extra charge in me. Like I didn’t need as much rest as everyone else, or like slowing down was the thing that would mess me up.\n\nBut it wasn’t just happy, either. That’s the part that makes me a little uncomfortable to admit. I was also way more touchy. If a coworker made some little comment, or if someone slowed me down when I was trying to get something done, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Like, I’d hear myself getting sharp, and part of me would be like, “Why are you reacting like that?” but in the moment it felt completely justified, like they were in my way.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I did feel unusually energized or high, but it came with this edge to it too. Like really up, but also really easy to set off.\n\nDoctor: During those stretches when you felt especially energized, did you feel unusually confident or powerful, like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Like in class or on job sites, when instructors or the senior guys were explaining how something should be done, I’d be sitting there thinking, “Okay, but why are we doing it that way when there’s obviously a better way?” And not just, like, a normal suggestion. It was more like I genuinely thought I could design something better than them — better than people who had been doing it for years.\n\nThere was this whole idea in my head that if people would just listen to me, I could change how every building in Miami handled water. Like I was right on the edge of figuring out something huge, and everyone else was moving too slow or being stuck in old habits.
